       02:46  Go through each account and sum dollars per asset class (total $268,431)
       03:53  Current % = class $ / total (absolute reference)
       05:38  Desired $ = target % x total; difference = desired - current (e.g. -$6,702 US stocks, +$6,200 intl, +$8,200 bonds, -$5,700 RE, -$2,000 gold)
       07:18  Conditional formatting bands using the 5/25 rule: +/-5 points for large classes (25-35%, 20-30%), +/-25% relative for small ones (11.25-18.75% for 15%, 3.75-6.25% for 5%)
       10:05  Conclusion: all classes inside bands, so no rebalance needed this time
    checks:
      - Targets sum to 100%
      - Band test per asset class (green/red)
    judgment calls:
      - Band width choice (5/25 rule)
      - Whether to act when inside bands
    maps to our data: none
    why useful: Minimal canonical drift-vs-target + tolerance-band spreadsheet; good baseline spec for a benchmark task's first stage.

## Workflow: Private equity fund performance analysis for LPs (TVPI/DPI/IRR, fund evaluation, pacing)

[B] PE Fund Performance Metrics: TVPI vs. MOIC vs. DPI and Gross vs. Net IRR
    https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bGhv_Kox5RA
    channel: Mergers & Inquisitions / Breaking Into Wall Street | length: 17:51 | uploaded: 20260121 | transcript read: True
    persona: Financial-modeling trainer (Mergers & Inquisitions / BIWS) teaching PE fund performance analysis for funds-of-funds/secondaries roles
    source documents:
      - Annual fund cash flows: invested capital per portfolio company, realizations, unrealized values
      - Fund terms: 2% management fee on commitments in investment period then 1.5% on remaining cost; 20% carry
    deliverable: Excel schedule of gross MOIC/IRR, management fees, realized and accrued carry, net value, NAV, TVPI, DPI, RVPI, net IRR with interpretation (fund 'solid but not spectacular', DPI 2x acceptable in year 6 of 10)
    steps:
       00:32  Before metrics, establish fund life-cycle stage, strategy and valuation assumptions
       01:40  Gross MOIC (~1.9x) = (realized + unrealized) / invested; gross IRR on gross cash flows
       03:13  Management fees: 2% of commitments in investment period, then 1.5% of remaining cost basis (~$4.7bn) in year 6
       04:17  Net value = gross value - realized and accrued carry; TVPI = net value / called capital (investments + fees)
       05:25  Net IRR on net cash flows (always below gross); DPI = (realizations - distributed carry) / called capital
       06:27  Interpretation: unknown valuation assumptions; DPI low but year 6 of 10; gross-net gap ~6-7 points normal; net IRR above long-run S&P but not by much
       07:57  Allocate management fees to companies by time-weighted cost-basis share (~$62-63m to the exited company)
       09:13  Carry per realized company = 20% x (realizations - investment - allocated fees), MIN(0,...) so losses do not create negative carry; accrued carry on unrealized pool, net of carry already paid
       12:05  Build gross and net cash-flow rows; NAV = unrealized value - accrued carry; compute MOIC, IRR, TVPI, DPI, RVPI = TVPI - DPI
       14:18  Complications: challenge GP marks (cut an 8-9x revenue multiple to 7-8x vs public comps), hurdle/catch-up waterfalls, recycling, clawbacks
    checks:
      - Net IRR < gross IRR once fees exist
      - Carry sign/MIN(0) guard for losses
      - RVPI = TVPI - DPI identity
      - Sanity-check GP valuation multiples against public comps
    judgment calls:
      - Whether a low DPI is a red flag given fund age
      - Haircutting GP marks
      - Fee allocation method
    maps to our data: Pension PE quarterly reports (e.g. WSIB: commitment, paid-in, distributions, NAV, IRR, TVPI per fund)
    why useful: Gives the exact LP metric definitions, formulas and interpretive rules an LP analyst applies to quarterly PE program data.

[B] PE Funds of Funds and Secondaries Case Studies: Full Prep Guide
    https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Md8aJNGavDo
    channel: Mergers & Inquisitions / Breaking Into Wall Street | length: 17:20 | uploaded: 20251210 | transcript read: True
    persona: Financial-modeling trainer (M&I/BIWS) describing PE fund-of-funds and secondaries analyst case studies (pension/endowment/SWF allocators)
    source documents:
      - GP track record: portfolio-company cash flows, entry/exit dates, deal size, industry, realized vs unrealized status across prior funds
      - For LP-led secondaries: unrealized companies' financials, GP marks, business plans, bid price/discount and deferrals
    deliverable: Yes/no commitment recommendation on a new fund with risk factors; for secondaries, a price/bid-discount recommendation that meets ~20% net IRR / 2x TVPI targets with sensitivities
    steps:
       01:05  Why LPs do this: pensions/endowments/SWFs evaluate GPs' new funds; LPs sell stakes for liquidity/rebalancing
       06:58  Primary fund case type 1: given cash flows, categorize and compute MOIC, IRR, net IRR, TVPI, DPI by company; recompute after an extra investment/distribution (XLOOKUP, SUMIFS, SUMPRODUCT, FILTER)
       07:38  Case type 2: evaluate historical funds and make a yes/no recommendation on the new fund
       08:10  Test consistency, stability and distribution of returns; avoid dependence on one sector, deal type or person; identify the GP's edge
       09:13  Fund I/II IRRs 20-25%, MOIC 2.3-3.6x look good; Fund III too young to judge
       09:46  Drill-down: strong in healthcare/consumer, weak in business services; deal size neutral; realized >> unrealized (less upside left); returns propped up by single 40%+ IRR deals -> list as risk factors
       10:51  Other cuts: by team member, by sourcing (proprietary vs banker-led); real data is scarce - CalPERS/CalSTRS publish fund-level data
       11:24  LP-led secondary: forecast unrealized companies, cut aggressive GP marks (car wash at 15x EBITDA -> 13.5x), deduct carry and fees, solve for price
       13:01  Sensitize returns to exit timing and bid discount; recommend if ~20% net IRR and 2x TVPI are reachable
    checks:
      - Realized vs unrealized split
      - Concentration of returns in a few deals
      - GP marks vs public comps
      - Sensitivity of net IRR/TVPI to exit timing and discount
    judgment calls:
      - Commit / do not commit to a new fund
      - Which risk factors are disqualifying vs mitigable
      - Price to bid for an LP stake
    maps to our data: Pension PE quarterly reports (WSIB/CalPERS/CalSTRS fund-level data) - explicitly cited as the public data source for practice
    why useful: Describes the LP analyst's due-diligence deliverable (commitment recommendation with risk factors) and the slicing they do; bridges W2 (manager selection) and W4.

[B] Private Markes Pacing Model - Basic: Overview
    https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xtCXm7t8WZ0
    channel: IRRModels | length: 10:31 | uploaded: 20250414 | transcript read: True
    persona: Private-markets modeler (IRRModels) presenting an LP commitment-pacing / cash-flow forecasting model
    source documents:
      - Per-fund commitment and assumptions (fund life, Takahashi-Alexander 'bow', yield, growth) or manual call/distribution patterns
      - Quarterly actuals per fund: capital calls, distributions, NAV
      - GP/sponsor grouping
    deliverable: Portfolio-level projected and actual calls, distributions, NAV and KPIs (IRR to date, DPI, TVPI) by fund and by GP, plus variance analysis vs initial projections
    steps:
       00:00  Structure: green summary sheets, blue assumptions, one tab per fund, technical checks sheet
       01:07  Fund tab: KPI summary, inputs, quarterly actuals input block (model is annual, actuals quarterly), standard pacing chart
       01:39  Rolling forecast = actuals to date + re-projected future; initial projections retained for tracking; a transition block bridges actuals to projections
       02:48  Choose call pacing (manual or curve) and distributions (manual or Takahashi-Alexander using fund life, bow, yield, growth)
       03:55  Enter year-end actuals for 2025-2026; all actual cells must be populated (red warning); model re-forecasts when actual calls/distributions run faster
       05:08  Charts: IRR to date and DPI; commitments, calls and total value
       05:43  Projected/actual summary tabs: fund-by-fund overview, portfolio chart, cash flows by fund, grouping by sponsor to evaluate GPs
       06:52  Variance analysis: initial projection vs actual calls, NAV, distributions, KPIs
       08:03  Add a fund by duplicating a tab; checks sheet flags that the new fund is not mapped to a GP until rows are added
    checks:
      - All actuals populated
      - Checks sheet: every fund mapped to a GP/summary
      - Variance vs initial projection
    judgment calls:
      - Pacing-curve parameters (bow, yield, growth)
      - How to blend actuals with projections
      - GP grouping
    maps to our data: Pension PE quarterly reports (WSIB fund-level calls/distributions/NAV as the 'actuals' input)
    why useful: Commitment pacing is a recurring pension/endowment workflow; this shows the workbook anatomy (actuals + T-A projections + checks) a benchmark task could require.

## Workflow: ABS/CMBS surveillance & modeling

[B] CMBS Webinar Series: Trends Observed in CMBS Surveillance
    https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LBnE1qMIe_k
    channel: Morningstar DBRS | length: n/a | uploaded: n/a | transcript read: None
    persona: Rating-agency CMBS surveillance analyst (Morningstar DBRS)
    source documents:
      - Monthly servicer remittance / loan-level reporting for conduit CMBS trusts (special-servicing status, watchlist, DSCR, occupancy, delinquency, interest shortfalls by class)
      - Special-servicer appraisals (as-is / stabilized values) and rent rolls
      - Deal capital structure (class balances, first-loss piece, defeasance, paydown since issuance)
      - Named conduit deals discussed: a 2015 conduit (captioned CCL 2015-C2), COMM 2014-CCRE20, Wells Fargo 2014-LC16, a 2012-C10 deal, GSMS 2011-GC5
    deliverable: Committee rating action per class (downgrade / confirm / negative trend) with written rationale, driven by hypothetical-liquidation losses
    steps:
       01:38  Portfolio-level trend read: special-servicing rate by property type (hotel/retail spike) used instead of delinquency buckets because forbearances broke the 30-60-90 roll
       06:47  Catalogue loan workouts: modifications vs forbearance vs 'other' (reserve suspensions, waived cash-trap triggers); track realized losses / liquidations
       09:30  Re-review every conduit deal; note data gaps (slow appraisals, no full-year financials, foreclosure moratoria)
       14:53  Per deal: pool snapshot from latest remittance - collateral reduction since issuance, # loans, property-type concentration, % on watchlist, % in special servicing, delinquent loans
       16:00  Per specially-serviced loan: pull latest appraisal vs issuance value, occupancy, DSCR, servicer commentary; apply haircut to appraisal and run a hypothetical liquidation -> loss severity
       23:28  Aggregate liquidation losses and run them up the capital stack from the unrated first-loss class; check which rated classes are eroded; track interest shortfalls creeping up the stack (e.g. class E receiving 83% of scheduled interest)
       36:33  Implied LTV on new appraisal (e.g. 226% vs 65% at issuance); single-loan losses can wipe out G/F/E and reach BBB(low) class D
       44:46  Judgment where no new appraisal: haircut issuance value (e.g. sale price evidence), consider maturity/refi risk for loans maturing within ~1 year
       47:28  Mitigants that support confirmations: strong loans (DSCR 3.56x), thick first-loss piece, paydown and defeasance increasing credit support
       50:11  Rating actions: downgrade N classes, change trends, confirm others; later back-test estimated loss vs realized loss in next remittance
    checks:
      - Back-test hypothetical liquidation loss against realized loss in subsequent remittance (2.9m estimate vs 2.4m realized)
      - Credit support per class recomputed after paydown/defeasance
      - Interest shortfall tracking by class as a leading indicator
    judgment calls:
      - Which loans to hypothetically liquidate vs leave performing
      - Size of haircut to latest appraisal / issuance value
      - Weight on maturity-default risk for upcoming balloons
      - Downgrade vs negative trend vs confirm for each class
    maps to our data: #3 (10-D distribution/servicer reports + ABS-EE loan-level CMBS data + 424B capital structure)
    why useful: Only video found where a structured-finance practitioner walks the real surveillance loop on named CMBS deals: remittance data -> loan-level liquidation scenarios -> loss allocation up the stack -> per-class rating action. Directly defines a benchmark task on EDGAR CMBS 10-D/ABS-EE data.

[B] Securitization Modeling in Excel
    https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cl0_H1NeYjY
    channel: SmartHelping | length: 38:50 | uploaded: 20250303 | transcript read: None
    persona: Financial-model template builder for lenders/securitization desks (SmartHelping)
    source documents:
      - Pool-level rep-line stats (initial balance, weighted average coupon, WAM/WART, CPR, CDR or default curve, recovery rate and lag)
      - Tranche terms (A/B/C notes + equity: size, fixed/floating coupon, index forward curve, payment frequency, PIK/accrual toggle)
      - Fees (servicing, trustee/admin), exit month and sale factor, OC and IC trigger levels
    deliverable: Securitization cash-flow model: collateral cash flows, sequential waterfall, per-tranche IRR/multiple, equity IRR sensitivity table (default rate x WAC), OC/IC trigger tests and charts
    steps:
       02:14  Tab layout: inputs/assumptions, returns, collateral cash flows, summary, waterfall, index curve, default curve, recoveries
       06:04  Enter securitized note funding vs collateral balance -> initial overcollateralization
       07:10  Principal amortization by simple % paydown or WAM-based level-pay synthetic loan; defaults net of recoveries reduce the amortization basis
       15:58  Convert annual CDR to monthly: 1-(1-CDR)^(1/12); recoveries = 50% after 6-month lag
       18:11  Prepayments via CPR converted to SMM
       19:17  Deduct servicing/trustee fees from interest to get available interest
       20:24  Sequential waterfall: interest to all classes pro rata by priority; 100% principal to class A until retired, then B, then C, residual to equity; unpaid interest accrues/compounds if toggled
       28:12  Stress: raise CDR to 12-20% and observe interest shortfall and principal loss on class C (63c recovery)
       31:33  OC test (collateral balance / notes outstanding) and IC test each period, flagged red/green
       37:43  Notes on alternative structures: pro-rata principal with turbo/sequential switch when OC trigger breached
    checks:
      - Author catches a mis-anchored $ reference in the floating-rate index lookup during the walkthrough (formula audit)
      - OC/IC ratios each period
      - Equity IRR sensitivity grid as sanity check
    judgment calls:
      - Simple-% vs WAM amortization method
      - Default/prepay/recovery assumptions
      - Exit month and sale price factor
    maps to our data: #3 (would consume 424B structure + ABS-EE pool stats; video uses synthetic inputs)
    why useful: Concrete anatomy of the workbook a structured-finance analyst builds; gives the formula-level conventions (CDR->MDR, CPR->SMM, sequential pay, OC/IC tests) a benchmark grader can check.

[B] Basic RMBS Model (Part I)
    https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=05oJHPqI5Do
    channel: AT Real Estate | length: 11:11 | uploaded: 20220921 | transcript read: None
    persona: Junior real-estate/structured-finance modeler (AT Real Estate), following Keith Allman 'Modeling Structured Finance Cash Flows'
    source documents:
      - Pool assumptions: balance, weighted-average fixed rate or floating index curve (1/3/6-mo LIBOR), 360-month term, prepayment and gross cumulative loss stresses
      - Liability structure: senior + subordinate tranche, margins, periodic/lifetime caps and floors, reset frequency, fees, principal reserve
    deliverable: Two-tranche RMBS cash-flow model with control panel, cash-flow snapshot, integrity tests and goal-seek for break-even collateral rate
    steps:
       01:46  Control panel: dynamic (blue) vs static (black) inputs; cash-flow snapshot to inspect any period
       02:21  Integrity tests: cash in = cash out; asset balance zero at maturity; senior/sub balances zero at maturity
       03:28  Goal Seek the weighted-average fixed rate that just retires the subordinate tranche; switch payment frequency and re-solve (+~100bp)
       05:42  Amortization sheet: inertial (scheduled) amortization then actual pool balance reduced by defaults, prepayments, amortization
       06:15  Waterfall: senior interest -> senior principal -> sub interest -> sub principal; unpaid interest accrues; principal reserve funded monthly
      P2 01:47  Floating-rate liabilities: margin, periodic cap, lifetime cap/floor, reset lag implemented on index curve
      P2 06:40  Stress tests: higher prepayment cuts senior interest and creates sub losses; higher defaults hit the sub tranche mostly
    checks:
      - Cash-in equals cash-out reconciliation
      - Balances zero at maturity
      - Interest tracker zeroes when shortfalls cured
    judgment calls:
      - Sequential interest-then-principal per tranche vs CMBS-style all-interest-first ordering
      - Which stresses to run
    maps to our data: #3 (RMBS/auto ABS structures from 424B; synthetic inputs in video)
    why useful: Explicit list of the tie-out tests a structured model must pass - good template for automatic grading of a waterfall workbook.

## Workflow: Credit agreement analysis, covenant compliance & borrowing base

[B] Line of Credit Basics - Part 2 - Understanding Borrowing Bases and Accounts Receivable Aging Reports
    https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jAK_XQszLhY
    channel: Capital Interactive | length: 42:58 | uploaded: 20210308 | transcript read: None
    persona: Commercial bankers / lending advisors (Capital Interactive: Adam Bosnjak & Besim Tafilaj) reviewing a client's AR aging and borrowing base certificate
    source documents:
      - Client AR aging report (customer-level: total, current, 30, 60, 90+ buckets; ~$17.9m total)
      - Bank borrowing base certificate template (commitment amount, ineligibles, advance rates, inventory, loan balance)
      - Loan agreement eligibility terms (cross-age %, concentration limit, foreign/government/affiliate exclusions, advance rates)
    deliverable: Completed borrowing base certificate: eligible AR -> availability after advance rate -> lesser of availability or commitment -> excess/(overadvance) vs outstanding loan; monthly
    steps:
       06:04  Start from total AR on the aging; scan the Total column for concentrations (any customer >20-25% of AR)
       08:14  Concentration judgment: tenure of customer, contracts, multiple contracts -> allow up to ~30% or cut/exclude
       11:27  Clarify what 'current' means in this client's aging (0-30 vs not-yet-due); remove >90 days (~$4.9m -> eligible base ~$12m)
       13:39  Cross-aging: if >=25% (sometimes 20%) of a customer's balance is >90 days, the customer's entire balance becomes ineligible (Lois Clark: 50% past 90 -> strip remaining $1.05m too)
       17:55  Customer-type review: individuals vs businesses, intercompany/owner receivables, foreign AR without credit insurance, government AR -> ineligible
       25:30  BBC mechanics: total AR 18m -> less >90, contras, concentrations, cross-aged, foreign, government, affiliate; add back approved >90 credits (e.g. named obligors allowed to 120 days)
       34:06  Apply advance rate (typically 80%) -> ~8m AR availability; inventory: total less ineligible (WIP) x ~50% advance, capped relative to AR
       36:59  Maximum loan amount = lesser of collateral availability and commitment ($2m); subtract outstanding balance -> remaining availability or required paydown
    checks:
      - Aging total ties to certificate starting AR
      - Ineligibles reconcile from total to eligible
      - Availability capped at commitment
      - Outstanding loan vs availability (overadvance -> paydown)
    judgment calls:
      - Concentration allowance by customer quality
      - Whether long-dated (gov't, oil & gas, healthcare) receivables get >90-day credit
      - Treatment of foreign AR with/without insurance
    maps to our data: #10 (credit agreements filed as EX-10 define eligibility, advance rates and borrowing base; ABL compliance certificates)
    why useful: Step-by-step bank-side computation of a borrowing base from a raw AR aging with the eligibility rules a credit agreement specifies - a clean, gradeable workflow.

    https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=oYkEAc8KTAw
    channel: Leo Correia | length: 10:00 | uploaded: 20191011 | transcript read: None

## Workflow: Restructuring: 13-week cash flow / DIP-style budget & variance

[B] Cash Forecasting to Stay Afloat: 13 Week Cash Flow Process
    https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hTAqXFQajRU
    channel: Grow It Forward | length: 14:56 | uploaded: 20200414 | transcript read: None
    persona: PE-portfolio / interim CFO (John Kahn, CPA/CA) running lender-facing 13-week cash forecasts
    source documents:
      - Bank-account / business-unit level receipts and disbursements (one locked input sheet per account or unit)
      - AR/collections outlook from customer-facing staff; AP, payroll, rent, debt service schedules
      - Credit-facility availability (ABL borrowing base), intercompany transfers
      - Prior week's forecast (for variance)
    deliverable: Rolling 13-week cash forecast report (week 0 actual-vs-forecast + 13 forward weeks) sent weekly to corporate/lenders, with minimum-cash and available-liquidity lines and design notes
    steps:
       04:23  Adopt lender/PE convention: 13 weeks, each week separate, refreshed weekly; fast-track the template and agree format with local teams
       05:28  One identical locked input sheet per bank account/BU; consolidate with 3-D formulas; schedule all payments on Friday so week-end is the low point
       06:55  Receipts: don't extrapolate history; involve people closest to customers; conservative in early weeks; chase one-offs (revolver draws, government programs, insurance)
       08:25  Payments: flex variable costs with volume; stop leakage; request deferrals/moratoria from landlords, vendors, lenders; use the 13-week to open lender relief talks
       09:38  Weekly update: compare last week's forecast to actual line by line; roll forward timing differences without double counting; refresh; iterate to stay cash-positive
       10:25  Reasonableness test: compare prior forecast's weekly balances to new forecast for same weeks and explain major differences
       12:18  Sample report layout: opening cash, receipts by line + subtotal, payments by line + subtotal, net, ending cash, change vs last week's forecast
       13:13  Checks: affiliate/intercompany transfers net to zero; minimum ending cash per unit; add forecast revolver availability only if collateral (ABL) supports it -> net available liquidity
    checks:
      - Intercompany/affiliate transfer activity must net to zero
      - Prior-forecast vs actual variance line by line (week 0 column)
      - Prior-forecast vs new-forecast balance comparison per week
      - Liquidity shown only if borrowing-base collateral supports the draw
    judgment calls:
      - Conservatism of early-week receipts
      - Which payments to defer and which relief to request
      - Minimum cash buffer per unit
      - Whether a shortfall is a one-week spike (timing) or structural (needs financing)
    maps to our data: #8 (Chapter 11 dockets: DIP budgets, budget-vs-actual variance reports and MORs follow this exact format)
    why useful: Practitioner description of the weekly forecast->actual->variance->reforecast loop and its built-in checks; the format is what DIP lenders require, so it maps onto docket variance reports.

[B] 13-Week Cash Flow Forecast in Excel | Full Model Walkthrough
    https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ayNHfj3i9gI
    channel: Excel with Ebrima | length: 14:34 | uploaded: 20261008 | transcript read: None
    persona: FP&A/advisory practitioner (Copperline Finance Partners) walking a full 13-week workbook
    source documents:
      - Bank/ERP export of dated collections and payments (pasted into a data tab)
      - Opening bank balance; AR and AP outlook; payroll, rent, taxes, debt service, capex, distributions
      - Minimum cash buffer, alert threshold, available credit facility; base/downside scenario selector
    deliverable: One-page 13-week summary (PDF to bank/private equity) + dashboard (lowest balance week, shortfall chart) + forecast-accuracy tab
    steps:
       01:07  Structure: exactly 13 weeks with dynamic +7-day dates; opening balance must equal bank balance
       02:14  Inflows: customer receipts (AR collected), other operating receipts (asset sales), financing draws
       03:20  Outflows: vendors (AP), payroll, supplies, rent, taxes, capex (cash-only items not visible on P&L)
       04:58  Net weekly cash flow -> closing balance = opening + net; conditional formatting OK/WATCH vs minimum buffer
       06:40  Levers when tight: accelerate collections, stretch DPO on controllable payables, or draw on the facility weeks in advance
       07:50  Commentary for leadership/PE: misses vs prior weeks, draws triggered, upcoming risks
       08:22  Update yellow inputs (model date, scenario) and paste bank/ERP transaction dump; formulas pull automatically

       11:09  Dashboard: current cash, projected ending, lowest balance and date, shortfall/draw chart
    checks:
      - Exactly 13 weekly columns with dynamic dates
      - Opening cash ties to bank
      - Forecast-vs-actual accuracy % by line category
      - Model checks tab
    judgment calls:
      - Diagnosing low inflow accuracy (contract terms, invoicing timeliness, collections)
      - When to draw on the line (5 weeks out, not 2)
    maps to our data: #8 (DIP budget / variance-report format)
    why useful: Shows the full workbook anatomy, including the variance/accuracy tab that is the core of DIP variance reporting.

## Workflow: Restructuring: recovery / waterfall analysis

[B] Financial Restructuring Mini Course - 05 of 11 - Out of Court Sample Plan
    https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8cigR2Ud6b8
    channel: Wall Street Prep | length: 10:23 | uploaded: 20170125 | transcript read: None
    persona: Restructuring trainer (Wall Street Prep financial restructuring mini-course) - in-court vs out-of-court plan analysis
    source documents:
      - Distressed company balance sheet & claims by class: 1L debt 100k, 2L debt 400k, unsecured notes 200k, trade payables 110k, 120k existing shares
      - DCF-based enterprise value (452k, incl. ~250k cash)
      - Proposed out-of-court plan terms (claim haircuts + new shares issued per class)
    deliverable: Plan evaluation: solvency after exchange, post-reorg equity value and ownership %, aggregate recovery % by class, opinion on which classes accept/reject
    steps:
       00:00  Lay out proposed plan: 40% haircut 1L, 60% haircut 2L + 400k shares, 60% haircut unsecured + 200k shares, trade at 50c
       01:34  Frame 4 questions: does the plan cure insolvency; aggregate recovery per claim; new equity ownership; is it a good proposal
       02:38  Post-exchange liabilities = 355k vs EV 452k -> implied equity 97k; net debt/EV ~38%
       04:50  Recovery by class = new debt + share of equity value: shares/720k total -> 2L owns 56% -> 53.9k equity + 160k debt = ~53% on 400k; unsecured ~53%; trade 50%; 1L 60%
       07:36  Ownership: 2L majority, unsecured 28%, old equity diluted to ~17%
       07:49  Judgment vs bankruptcy alternative: 1L is over-collateralized (452k assets incl. 250k cash) and would recover par in court -> rejects; old equity retaining 17% violates priority logic
       09:44  Conclusion: out-of-court terms are bounded by expected in-court (absolute priority) recoveries - leads into priority/APR waterfall (part 06) and revised plan (part 08)
    checks:
      - Post-restructuring EV - liabilities = equity value
      - Shares sum to total (720k) and ownership sums to 100%
      - Recovery per class compared to what APR waterfall would give in court
    judgment calls:
      - Whether residual leverage (38% net debt/EV) is sustainable
      - Which classes will accept
      - Whether 2L deserves priority over unsecured
    maps to our data: #8 (disclosure statements' recovery tables and liquidation analyses on Chapter 11 dockets)
    why useful: Clean, Excel-backed worked example of recovery-by-class math (debt + equity value split) and the accept/reject judgment; mirrors the recovery table in a disclosure statement.


====================================================================================================
10. INSIGHTS FROM THE VIDEOS
====================================================================================================

Synthesis across all four clusters (from transcripts)
  1. Tie out before analysing. Every serious workflow first reconciles inputs to a source of truth: net income
     to the 10-K (Otis), account totals to the custodian (rebalancing), detailed CAS rather than summary (India),
     prior-year closing balances to the issued report (government, pensions). Planted input inconsistencies make
     natural grading hooks.
  2. Data entry is a large share of the work. ~38% of the Otis working time is entering and tying historicals;
     ACFR and CalPERS videos hand-key numbers from PDFs that cannot be copied, and catch errors by recomputing
     totals against the report's own totals. Benchmarks that hand agents clean tables skip this.
  3. The key judgment is often the basis of a number: bond-document vs GAAP coverage (one airport: $10M vs $40M),
     fund vs government-wide statements, measurement date vs fiscal year, 990 total vs unrestricted columns,
     gross vs net IRR, blended vs headline benchmark.
  4. Deliverables are threshold decisions, and "no action" is a common correct answer: 5/25 rebalancing bands,
     top-quartile in 2 of the last 3-4 years, fund balance 10-15% of expenditures, one funder above 25-30% of
     revenue, ~20% net IRR / 2x TVPI.
  5. Costs, taxes and benchmark choice separate experts from novices (buy-only vs full rebalance, harvesting
     losses first, fee drag, gross-to-net bridges).
  6. Analysts start from last year's work and roll forward: exactly the multi-period structure the benchmark
     needs.
  7. Some steps are rule-based and auto-gradable (GASB entries, 5% foundation payout, single-audit program
     rules, balance checks); credit views, recommendations and memos need a rubric.
  8. Full-chain videos are rare (8 of 71). Most show one slice (reading documents, or analysing numbers already
     in a sheet). No video shows: a municipal credit model and memo, an ABS surveillance workbook on a real deal,
     analysis of Chapter 11 monthly operating reports, a bank Call Report review, or a consultant's fund
     due-diligence memo from source documents. Public pension board meetings show the deliverable, never the
     analyst's workbook.


----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Public sector & nonprofit: lessons (from docs/videos/notes_public.md)
----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
## Cross-cutting lessons for benchmark design

1. Work starts from last year's document. ACFR preparers, closers and auditors begin by tying beginning balances to the prior issued report and rolling the template forward. Strong Towns fills ratio series from the newest report backward to pick up restatements. Tasks should supply a prior-year artifact and grade the roll-forward.
2. Manual PDF extraction is the norm and the main error source. Both the Strong Towns ACFR and the CalPERS allocation schedules are explicitly "not copyable" and are keyed by hand. Practitioners guard against errors with unit settings, re-reading numbers, and recomputed totals checked against report totals. A benchmark should plant tie-out totals.
3. Basis traps are the core judgment. Several pairs of figures look alike but are measured differently:
   - GAAP vs bond-document vs rate-setting "revenues" and "coverage" (operating income $10M vs net revenues $40M)
   - fund (modified accrual) vs government-wide (accrual) statements
   - measurement date vs fiscal year-end (the two-year CalPERS rate lag)
   - 990 total vs without-donor-restriction columns
   - tax period vs form year
   - official statement date vs the fiscal year of its data
4. Workflows mix deterministic steps (gradable) with judgment (rubric). The deterministic ones are the GASB 34 conversion entries, NIICA, GASB 54 classes, the major-program four steps, the GASB 68 deferral mechanics, 990-PF payout and opinion-unit scope filtering. The judgment-heavy ones are the credit view (Anchorage downgrade logic), grant due diligence (concentration, pattern consistency) and pension paydown strategy.
5. Deliverables are specific:
   - rating presentation and recovery plan
   - government-wide statements and ACFR
   - GASB 68 journal entries and note tables
   - UAL paydown scenario table
   - due-diligence memo or investigative story with a multi-year 990 tracker
   - major-program and selection workpapers
6. Thresholds practitioners cite (useful for rubrics):
   - fund balance policy of 10% (+2-3%) of general fund expenditures (Anchorage); 15% for Michigan schools
   - interest / revenue of 2-3%
   - net book value / cost of capital assets around 60-65%
   - total assets / total liabilities above 1
   - single funder above 25-30% or one government contract above 40% of revenue
   - foundation payout of 5%
   - Type A $750K and 20% / 40% coverage (pre-2024 rules)
7. Data-source fit.
   - #1 ACFR PDFs anchor W1 and W2 and give answer keys for W3 (pension notes and RSI) and W2 (reconciliation schedules).
   - #7 990 XML maps directly to W4 (every Financial SCAN or ProPublica metric is computable from 990 XML Parts VIII-X and the schedules).
   - #2 ledgers/checkbooks fit W2 year-end close and W5 disbursement testing, but no video uses public checkbook data. That is the clearest gap where the benchmark would be original rather than mirrored.

----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Funds & wealth: lessons (from docs/videos/notes_funds.md)
----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
## Cross-cutting lessons for benchmark design

### 1. Practitioners tie out before they analyze

Every serious workflow starts by reconciling inputs to a source of truth:

- Account totals are matched to the custodian (rebalancing).
- The detailed CAS is used rather than the summary CAS (India reviews).
- Prior-day cash and positions must agree before today's breaks are worked (fund accounting).
- The balance sheet must tie before NAV is struck.
- Policy-range tables are corrected before compliance is asserted (pension).

Benchmark tasks should plant input inconsistencies (missing cash in a lot file, stale fiscal year-ends, a typo in the policy range) and grade whether the agent catches them.

### 2. Deliverables are decisions with thresholds, and "no action" is a common correct answer

The thresholds used are explicit:

- Rebalancing bands (5/25 rule, 20% relative band, mid+small cap at 55% ±5).
- Watch-list rules (top-quartile category rank in 2 of the last 3-4 years, fi360 top 25%).
- Exit triggers (fails both the benchmark and category-rank tests, manager change, regular-plan commission).
- PE commitment targets (about 20% net IRR and 2x TVPI).
- Pension allocations kept within about 1% of targets or within the policy min/max.

Several real reviews end in "no changes recommended". Grading should reward a correct no-trade or no-change conclusion, not only activity.

### 3. Cost and tax layers are where the expertise shows

- Practitioners compare a trade-everything variant with a buy-only variant (avoid realizing gains) and with tax-neutral loss harvesting.
- They look through fund-of-funds to the underlying expense ratios.
- They add the advisory fee to the fund's TER.
- They separate SB 322 investment expense, which includes carry, from audited expense.
- They compute a gross-to-net bridge (fees and carry) for PE.

Tasks should require these layers, not just headline returns.

### 4. Benchmarks and time windows are judgment calls the analyst must justify

Examples seen:

- Blended benchmark vs Nifty 50 for a multi-cap portfolio.
- Total-return index for rolling returns.
- Policy benchmark vs a 65/35 public-market equivalent vs a peer universe vs the 7% actuarial bogey.
- PE vs public + 300 bp, lagged one quarter.
- 60/40 vs 50/50 chosen by a plan's target return.
- Fund age (DPI of 2x in year 6 of 10 is fine).
- Ranking windows that suit the client's holding period.

A good task gives the agent several plausible benchmarks and grades the stated rationale.

### 5. The real input formats are messy exports

- Schwab tax-lot CSVs without cash.
- CAMS/KFintech CAS PDFs.
- AMFI NAV text files with missing dates.
- Moneycontrol HTML tables.
- Custodian and fund-accounting cash ledgers by currency.
- Quarterly PE actuals (calls, distributions, NAV) per fund.

Our sources map cleanly:

| Source | What it feeds |
|---|---|
| #6 AMFI NAV/TER plus AMC monthly portfolios | India scheme analysis and client reviews (holdings look-through, overlap, rolling returns, regular-vs-direct cost) |
| #5 N-PORT | Fund-of-funds and holdings look-through, attribution weights, and seed positions for NAV/reconciliation tasks |
| Pension PE quarterly reports (WSIB) | LP metrics, pacing actuals, and unfunded-vs-NAV liquidity |
| Pension filings and #7 990-PF | The cross-entity oversight variant (5b) |

### 6. Human time scale

These are minutes-to-hours tasks per client or fund, repeated across a book. Evidence:

- The rebalancing calculator is run live in a client meeting.
- Parsing a CAS takes 7-10 minutes, then the review is a meeting.
- The trainer says the NAV/SIP case "takes half an hour".
- AUXILIA estimates 5-6 hours to build 12 conventional net-IRR models.
- Reconciliation is daily and break-driven.
- Quarterly pension reviews are 40-90 minute board sessions backed by weeks of staff and consultant work.

A long-horizon task can chain them: holdings ingest, then tie-out, then analysis, then the decision memo or trade file.

----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Anatomy synthesized per workflow: public sector
----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
## W1. Municipal bond credit analysis from ACFR / official statement

Synthesized anatomy (common sequence across the videos)
1. Assemble the document set. Get the latest ACFR and prior ACFRs, working backward from the most recent so that restated comparatives are used. Add the official statement for the bond and any rating reports. Sources: Strong Towns P6, Dafang Wu.
2. Triage document quality. Check the auditor's opinion (modified, going concern, restatement; outside firm or elected auditor). Note whether the government holds the GFOA certificate. Treat the MD&A as unaudited management narrative. Sources: TIA, Wu.
3. Extract government-wide figures. From the MD&A condensed tables, statement of net position and statement of activities, take assets, deferred outflows and inflows, liabilities, net position (especially unrestricted), revenues (operating and capital grants), and interest on debt. From the capital-asset note, take cost and accumulated depreciation. Units are fixed once and prior-year columns are entered at the same time. Sources: Strong Towns P5, TIA.
4. Extract long-term liabilities from notes and RSI. Net pension liability (sometimes only in the notes), funded ratio from the RSI schedule of changes, OPEB, and the debt schedule. Sources: TIA, Wu.
5. General-fund lens. Compute unrestricted (unassigned) general fund balance as a % of current-year expenditures and compare it with the government's written policy. This drives the rating agency's management and financial factors. Source: Anchorage.
6. Revenue bonds. Start with the rate covenant and the defined terms in the official statement. Compute coverage on the bond-document basis, which is not the GAAP basis. Check the additional-bonds test and the basis behind days cash on hand. Source: Wu.
7. Build a multi-year ratio series (10 years) and read trends against thresholds. Total assets / total liabilities below 1 means insolvent. Interest / revenue of 2-3% is manageable. Net book value / cost of capital assets around 60-65%. Track dependence on intergovernmental transfers and whether fund balance meets policy. Sources: Strong Towns P6, Anchorage.
8. Conclude in rating-factor form (S&P's seven factors), with a reconciliation of forecast to actual and a recovery plan. Source: Anchorage.

*Human time implied:* about 15 minutes of hand-keying per fiscal year (about 15 inputs), so 2-3 hours for a 10-year series. A GO bond sale and rating cycle runs 60-90 days.

Gaps: No video shows a buy-side or rating-agency muni analyst building a credit model in Excel from an ACFR and writing a credit memo. There is no on-screen calculation of debt per capita, overlapping debt, debt service as % of spending, or revenue-bond coverage. The closest pieces are the Strong Towns pair (extraction and interpretation), Anchorage (rating criteria) and Wu (reading order). A benchmark task here has to be designed from these parts rather than copied from one video.


---

## W2. Government fund accounting: GASB 34 conversion, fund balance, year-end close, budget-to-actual

Synthesized anatomy
1. Roll forward from the prior year. Agree beginning fund balance and net position in the trial balance to the prior issued ACFR, and post the auditor's late entries. Source: Forvis, Cherry Bekaert.
2. Reconcile through the year and at cutoff. Monthly balance-sheet reconciliations; accruals and the search for unrecorded liabilities; cash and investment confirmations; capital assets and CIP roll-forward (retainage); debt roll-forward and arbitrage; leases and SBITAs; pension and OPEB entries from actuarial packages. Source: Forvis.
3. Import the final trial balance. Map new accounts and rerun the major-fund test. Source: Cherry Bekaert.
4. Draft back to front. Combining schedules, then fund statements, then a GASB 34 conversion worksheet with these entries: capital assets and depreciation, removal of capital outlay, bond proceeds and principal reclassified, accrued interest, premium amortization, availability-based deferred inflows, interfund transfers eliminated, internal service fund folded in. Source: Farhat.
5. Classify equity. Net position: NIICA, restricted, unrestricted. Fund balance under GASB 54: nonspendable, restricted, committed, assigned, unassigned (only the general fund may show a positive unassigned balance). Sources: Farhat, LSSU, Cherry Bekaert.
6. Budgetary comparison (original, final, actual, variance) for the general fund and major special revenue funds; non-major funds go in other supplementary information. Budget groupings must match the ACFR. Sources: Forvis, Cherry Bekaert.
7. Notes, RSI, statistical section, MD&A (with reasons for changes).
8. QC. Check figures between statements zero out; MD&A ties to the statements; side-by-side analytical review against the prior year; GFOA checklist.

*Human time:* 39% of webinar respondents need more than 8 weeks for the ACFR and 25% need 5-8 weeks. The GFOA target is issuance within 6 months of fiscal year-end.

Gaps: No screen-share of a real city's budget-to-actual variance analysis in Excel; the search surfaced only corporate FP&A templates and CPA-exam encumbrance lectures. No real trial balance to ACFR walkthrough; Farhat uses a fictional county. Nothing uses state or city checkbook/ledger exports (#2) directly.


---

## W3. Public pension actuarial valuation / net pension liability (GASB 67/68)

Synthesized anatomy
1. Collect the documents. The plan's funding valuation; the GASB 67/68 accounting valuation and the employer allocation schedule; the employer's contribution records; prior-year notes. Sources: CalPERS tool, GovInvest.
2. Extract funded status, total pension liability / fiduciary net position / NPL, normal cost %, UAL payment, discount rate and ±1% sensitivity, and the amortization bases. Source: GovInvest.
3. Reconcile year over year. Expected vs actual UAL; asset gain or loss (6.7% actual vs 7% assumed); assumption, method and demographic changes; normal-cost drift (PEPRA). Source: GovInvest.
4. Handle timing. The measurement date differs from the fiscal year-end; rates lag two years; contributions made after the measurement date are deferred outflows. Sources: CalPERS tool, GovInvest.
5. Employer accounting. Proportionate share from the allocation factors; employer-specific deferrals (change in proportion, contributions vs proportionate share); journal entries and note/RSI tables. Source: CalPERS tool.
6. Decision analysis. Extra discretionary payments to long vs short bases, recurring payments to a level target, fresh starts, Section 115 trust, discount-rate sensitivity, with total payments and interest saved for each. Source: CalPERS UAL.
7. Output: journal entries and notes for the ACFR, plus a budget forecast or paydown memo.

Gaps: No independent analyst (Pew, Reason or rating-agency style) builds a state-plan stress test or contribution-adequacy model in Excel on screen. There is no plan-side GASB 67 report preparation. OPEB analogues exist only as accounting webinars (Maze, Cherry Bekaert), which were not read.


---

## W4. Nonprofit financial analysis from Form 990 / audited statements (grant due diligence, 990-PF)

Synthesized anatomy
1. Pull 3-5 years of 990s (ProPublica, Candid, IRS TEOS) plus audited statements. Check the tax period against the form year and use amended returns where filed. Sources: ProPublica, 990-PF lesson.
2. Build a tracking sheet. Revenue mix (Part VIII: contributions, government grants, program revenue); functional expenses (Part IX); balance sheet (Part X); compensation (Part VII, Schedule J); related parties (Schedules L and R); grants made (Schedule I, or 990-PF Part XV); endowment (Schedule D). Sources: ProPublica, Caltech.
3. Compute vital signs. Change in unrestricted net assets before and after depreciation (not the total, which mixes in restricted gifts); LUNA or estimated unrestricted liquid net assets; months of cash; liabilities/assets; % of buildings and equipment depreciated; revenue concentration (one funder above 25-30%, or one government contract above 40%); for 990-PF, payout against 5% of average fair market value. Sources: Financial SCAN, Wallace, grantee DD, 990-PF.
4. Benchmark against peers: NTEE code plus revenue band medians. Source: Financial SCAN.
5. Explain outliers before concluding. Read Schedule O and the supplemental information (for example, $10M "other compensation" turned out to be deferred compensation paid at retirement), plus notes on liquidity, allocation methodology and revenue recognition. Sources: ProPublica, grantee DD.
6. Deliverable: due-diligence recommendation, investigative story, or foundation prospect profile.

Gaps: No video shows a grantmaker analyst building a 990-to-Excel model end to end on screen. Financial SCAN is a paid tool doing it, and ProPublica's spreadsheet is described rather than shown. Endowment spending policy calculations (for example, a percentage of a trailing 12-quarter average) appear only in investment-consultant talks, which were not read. Some 990-PF grant analysis (Part XV medians) is covered in the course's next lesson, which was not fetched.


---

## W5. Single audit / government audit fieldwork (SEFA, major programs, sampling, tie-outs)

Synthesized anatomy
1. SEFA. Draft it early from the general ledger with these columns: agency, program, ALN, pass-through ID, expenditures, amounts to subrecipients, clusters, loans. Reconcile it quarterly to the GL and to grantor reports. Sources: Forvis, San Mateo, SEK.
2. Applicability. Compare federal *expenditures* in the fiscal year (not awards; vendor payments don't count) with the threshold. Sources: San Mateo, SEK.
3. Major-program determination. Type A/B threshold; risk-assess Type A programs; find high-risk Type B programs (at least 1/4 of the low-risk Type A count; skip programs under 25% of the Type A threshold); check coverage of 20% (low-risk auditee) or 40%. Source: CPA Hall Talk.
4. Compliance requirements per major program come from the Compliance Supplement matrix (at most 6 direct and material). Source: SEK.
5. Sampling and testing. One sample can serve controls and compliance (use the larger size). Attributes: allowability, period of performance (120-day liquidation), cash management (interest above $500 returned), procurement thresholds and debarment checks, reporting tied to support. Sources: SEK, San Mateo.
6. Financial-statement side. Materiality for each opinion unit; filter post-year-end disbursements by fund and scope for the search for unrecorded liabilities; tie the statements to the workpapers. Source: Harshwal.
7. Findings and reporting. Findings and questioned costs; corrective action plan; submission within 9 months of fiscal year-end. Source: San Mateo.

*Note:* the videos use pre-2024 Uniform Guidance thresholds ($750K single-audit threshold, $750K Type A floor). The 2024 revision raised the single-audit threshold to $1M for fiscal years beginning on or after Oct 1, 2024, so tasks must fix the regime by fiscal year.

Gaps: No screen-share of an actual compliance test workpaper (a sample of payroll or vouchers with attribute columns) or of preparing a SEFA in Excel. The Harshwal statement-to-workpaper tie-out video (ZTgdYJF1rNY) has no captions and was not verified.


---

----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Anatomy synthesized per workflow: funds & wealth
----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
## 1. Portfolio rebalancing (drift vs target, bands, trade list, tax-aware, model portfolios)

Inputs. Custodian positions per account (a tax-lot file for taxable accounts), cash (often missing from lot exports and keyed by hand), a security-to-asset-class map (Morningstar categories feed the platforms), model or target weights at asset-class or ticker level, tolerance bands, client cash needs (withdrawals or contributions), and each account's tax type (taxable, IRA, Roth).

Steps, in the order practitioners follow them.
1. Aggregate holdings by account and asset class, and tie the totals to the custodian.
2. Compute current weights and compare them with the targets.
3. Flag drift outside the bands. Examples seen: the 5/25 rule (±5 points absolute or ±25% relative) and a 20% relative band.
4. Decide whether to trade at all. Frequently nothing is outside the bands and the answer is "no trades".
5. Choose the trade style: full rebalance, partial (out-of-band classes only), or buy-only deployment of cash.
6. Apply the tax overlay: harvest losses first, select lots, and compare tax-neutral, tax-efficient and back-to-target scenarios.
7. Layer in cash needs and asset location across accounts.
8. Round to whole shares and minimum lots, which leaves residual cash.
9. Export a custodian-format trade file or blotter and confirm post-trade weights.
10. Keep monitoring drift.

Deliverable. A per-account trade list in dollars or shares, plus a household allocation and tax-location summary.

Recurring checks. Targets sum to 100%. Account totals tie to the custodian. Pre-trade total equals post-trade total. Post-trade weights land inside the bands. A flag appears when the requested cash cannot be raised without selling.

Time. The spreadsheet versions run in minutes once set up; the planner runs it live in a client meeting. The RIA's tax tool was "built in three sessions".

## 2. Fund/ETF due diligence & manager selection (holdings, attribution, fees, replacement decision)

Inputs. Fund NAV or returns, the benchmark and category peer set, holdings (sector, style and market cap), expense ratio, turnover, manager tenure and changes, and the model or menu in which the fund sits.

Steps.
1. Classify what the fund is: style box, index or active, US or foreign.
2. Measure return vs benchmark and vs category rank over several windows.
3. Compute risk statistics (beta, alpha, R², Sharpe, capture ratios).
4. Run attribution (Brinson allocation, selection and interaction) and identify detractors.
5. Compare costs. Expense ratio and turnover are converted to dollars per $10k, and the question is whether the fee is earned.
6. If a fund fails, screen its category for replacements.
7. Re-optimize the model with weight constraints.
8. Produce the proposal or monitoring report and push the model to the rebalancer.

Rules seen. Watch-list criteria are set in the IPS: an fi360 score in the top 25%, plus separate passive criteria (tracking error, information ratio, expense ratio) and separate criteria for target-date funds and money-market funds. One period of attribution is never enough to act on. A manager change is an explicit trigger.

Deliverable. A monitoring or proposal report, or a model change.

## 3a. India MF client portfolio review (CAS -> allocation/benchmark/category review -> exit/switch recommendation)

Inputs. The client's detailed CAS from CAMS or KFintech. Practitioners stress transaction-level data, not the NSDL/CDSL summary. Also the risk profile (capacity, tolerance and required risk), the agreed target allocation, a blended or category benchmark, and category rank or quartile data.

Steps.
1. Aggregate all folios and compute XIRR.
2. Compare the portfolio with a blended benchmark and with headline indices over several windows.
3. Check asset allocation vs target (equity/debt/gold/cash) and market-cap mix vs band. One example: mid plus small cap at 55% ±5.
4. Review each scheme vs its benchmark and category quartile. Keep a scheme if it sits in the top or upper-middle quartile in 2-3 of the last 3-4 years; minimum one-year track record.
5. Flag drags: inline or underperforming schemes, manager changes, regular plans paying commission.
6. Check look-through sector concentration (financials above ~30%) and overlap with direct equity.
7. Recommend exits or switches with the capital-gains impact.
8. Rebalance by booking profit in the overweight asset class.
9. Present a PDF to the client.

Deliverable. A client review report with an action list (exit, switch, rebalance).

## 3b. India MF scheme analysis from AMC/AMFI data (holdings extraction, overlap, rolling returns vs benchmark, factsheet reading)

Inputs. AMFI NAV history (growth option; regular plans give longer history), benchmark TRI series, and scheme holdings (aggregator pages or AMC monthly portfolio files).

Steps.
1. Extract holdings or NAV into the sheet (IMPORTHTML, macros, lookups) and handle missing dates.
2. Normalize NAV and compute rolling returns and rolling risk vs the benchmark.
3. Compute an outperformance-consistency statistic (share of windows beating the index).
4. Build SIP or lumpsum cash flows and compute XIRR per scheme and for the portfolio.
5. Run allocation sensitivity with a data table.
6. Compare holdings across funds for overlap.

Deliverable. Charts and statistics feeding a fund review or recommendation.

## 4. Private equity fund performance analysis for LPs (TVPI/DPI/IRR, fund evaluation, pacing)

Inputs. Fund cash flows from capital account statements or quarterly reports: commitment, paid-in, distributions and NAV per fund. Also fee terms, the GP's deal-level track record (entry and exit, industry, size, realized or unrealized), and pacing assumptions (Takahashi-Alexander: fund life, bow, yield, growth).

Steps.
1. Establish fund age and strategy first.
2. Compute gross MOIC and IRR, then the fee and carry schedule (time-weighted fee allocation, MIN(0) guard on carry).
3. Compute net TVPI, DPI, RVPI and net IRR; check that RVPI = TVPI - DPI and that net is below gross.
4. Interpret against life-cycle stage. A DPI of 2x in year 6 of 10 is acceptable.
5. Challenge GP marks against public comps.
6. For new commitments, slice the track record by fund, industry, deal size and realized vs unrealized, and test concentration in single deals.
7. Make the yes/no commitment call with risk factors.
8. Run pacing: feed quarterly actuals into the T-A projection, re-forecast, and run variance vs the initial plan, by fund and by GP.

Deliverable. A performance table and memo, a commitment recommendation, and a pacing plan.

Time. AUXILIA estimates 5-6 hours to build 12 conventional net-IRR cash-flow models, versus about 10 minutes with the effective-hold formula.

## 5. Pension / endowment asset allocation vs policy & quarterly performance review

Inputs. The IPS with targets and min/max ranges, quarter-end market values by asset class and manager, net returns vs the policy benchmark, a public-market-equivalent mix (e.g. 65/35), peer universe ranks, lagged private-markets returns vs premium benchmarks (PE = public + 300 bp, real estate = ODCE + 100 bp), actuarial assumed return, capital-market assumptions from the last SAA study, and risk-model output.

Steps.
1. Market context.
2. Allocation vs targets and ranges, including look-through of equity exposure in infrastructure and real estate against the equity cap.
3. Total-plan return vs policy, PME, peers and the actuarial bogey.
4. Explain the gaps: manager selection, deviation from targets, and the mismatch between private-market and public benchmarks.
5. Asset-class and manager excess returns.
6. Risk: expected range vs actual, ex-ante volatility, active risk, and a risk-allocation view. At NYC Fire, public plus private equity drove 81% of risk.
7. Private-markets liquidity: unfunded commitments vs NAV, a pacing plan with conservative distribution assumptions, and the cash needed for benefit payments.
8. Report the rebalancing trades executed. At NYC Fire, ~2.5% moved from US equity to non-US, TIPS were exited, and $220m was raised for benefits.
9. Recommendation. Usually "no changes"; the bar for changing allocation is high.
10. The board votes to accept the report.

Oversight variant (Texas PRB). Compare many systems' returns with their assumptions and with a passive benchmark chosen by target return. Group by fiscal year-end, flag stale reporters, compute the "drift benefit" from lowering return assumptions, and reconcile expense definitions.

## 5b. Pension oversight variant: cross-system review of returns vs actuarial assumptions, passive benchmarks and expenses (state regulator)

Oversight analyst workflow (Texas PRB).
1. Collect each system's annual returns by fiscal year-end, its actuarial assumed return now and 10 years ago, its broad asset allocation, and investment expenses (SB 322 definition including carry and profit share, vs the audited figure).
2. Compare returns with the assumption.
3. Add a passive benchmark chosen by target return (60/40, 50/50 or 40/60).
4. Group systems by fiscal year-end so the comparisons are apples to apples, and flag lagging reporters.
5. Compute a "drift benefit" that separates real outperformance from lowered assumptions.
6. Show the expense ratio. High cost with low return is the red flag.

Deliverable. A single cross-system Investment Data Report for the board.

## 6. Fund accounting: NAV calculation and custodian reconciliation (fund administrator)

Inputs. Fund-accounting ledger (positions, cash by currency, accruals), custodian statements, investment-manager instructions, broker statements for derivatives, corporate-action and withholding-tax vendor data, capital-stock activity from the transfer agent, and a trial balance.

Steps (daily or NAV cycle).
1. Confirm that prior-day FA and custodian balances agree.
2. Reconcile positions by security, then income accruals before the pay date, then cash line by line by currency.
3. Classify each break: timing (contractual vs actual settlement), failed trade, missing IM instruction, FX rate or date, asset set up in a different currency, corporate action, withholding tax, cancelled dividend, late instruction after the NAV cut-off.
4. Comment and chase each break the same day; ageing breaks become backlog.
5. Value positions at fair value and book accruals and expenses.
6. Confirm the balance sheet ties. A missing cash line blocks the NAV.
7. Strike NAV = (assets - liabilities) / units.
8. Test the high-water mark before accruing an incentive fee.
9. Book capital-stock activity at the correct NAV.
10. Assemble the NAV pack for reviewer and fund-manager sign-off.

Deliverable. Reconciled books, NAV per unit, and the NAV package.


====================================================================================================
11. WORKFLOWS WE CAN BUILD
====================================================================================================

Same level as the Otis reference (all seven operations), built on our real sources. The sequences are
proposals; video IDs point to the closest evidence (watch at https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=<id>).


* Municipal credit model and memo  [#1 annual-report PDFs + #2 ledgers; persona: Muni credit analyst]
    source documents: 5-10 years of a city's audited annual reports (PDF, some scanned), latest bond official statement, pension actuarial report
    workbook built  : Multi-year statements keyed in and tied to reported totals; fund balance, liquidity, debt service and pension ratios; 3-5 year projection
    deliverable     : Credit memo with a rating view and named risks (e.g. fund balance vs the city's own policy floor)
    checks vs source: Fund balance rolls forward; the report's reconciliation schedules; debt schedule vs debt note
    judgment calls  : Restatements and special items; coverage on the bond-document basis vs GAAP; which revenues recur
    closest videos  : yAbiyx0cgM8 (A), _Im0iXY2SgA, UE8RKZol3VA

* Utility rate-base and revenue-requirement model  [#4 FERC Form 1; persona: Regulatory analyst]
    source documents: 10 years of a utility's FERC Form 1 schedules plus its rate filing
    workbook built  : Plant and depreciation roll-forwards tied to the filing; rate base; cost of capital; revenue requirement; peer cost benchmarks
    deliverable     : Assessment of whether the requested rate increase is supported
    checks vs source: Roll-forwards tie (99.7%); totals vs the published rate-base table
    judgment calls  : Sign conventions; explaining restatement breaks; test-year normalisation
    closest videos  : YZ7B5BDuB74, xBkH28qfSaU

* Securitization deal surveillance model  [#3 loan tapes + servicer reports + prospectus; persona: Structured-credit analyst]
    source documents: Monthly loan-level tapes, monthly servicer reports, prospectus waterfall
    workbook built  : Strats, roll rates, prepay/default speeds, pool roll-forward, waterfall and trigger replication
    deliverable     : Surveillance note: performance vs pricing assumptions; projected trigger breaches
    checks vs source: Loan balances sum to reported pool balance; distributions match servicer report
    judgment calls  : Recoveries vs repossession proceeds; loans that drop out; forecast assumptions
    closest videos  : cl0_H1NeYjY, LBnE1qMIe_k, 05oJHPqI5Do

* Target-date fund look-through and rebalance  [#5 SEC N-PORT; persona: OCIO / wealth analyst]
    source documents: Quarterly N-PORT reports for a fund family and its underlying funds; prospectus glide path
    workbook built  : Look-through holdings, drift vs glide path, return chaining vs reported returns, fee drag, trade list
    deliverable     : Due-diligence note and rebalancing trades, or a reasoned 'no change'
    checks vs source: Holdings sum to net assets; chained returns match reported
    judgment calls  : Benchmark choice; rebalancing bands; treating the 2022 merger
    closest videos  : G6KoHtjdGio (A), ROAGmAgoMAE, rNvlfiNIh5Y

* India client portfolio review and switch plan  [#6 fund-house workbooks + AMFI NAV; persona: Distributor / adviser]
    source documents: Client's consolidated account statement, each fund house's monthly workbook (different layouts), NAV and TER history
    workbook built  : Holdings parsed per layout, look-through and overlap, category-rule checks, returns/risk from NAV, switch scenarios with tax
    deliverable     : Client review document with exits, switches and tax impact
    checks vs source: Holdings tie to each fund's grand total; units x NAV = statement value
    judgment calls  : Quartile vs benchmark tests; time window for recent investors; regular vs direct
    closest videos  : p_m2DCJi520 (A), 7S0VFj05N9k, 1zHh9I1IKkg

* Foundation year-end close and payout plan  [#7 IRS 990 / 990-PF; persona: Foundation CFO]
    source documents: Five years of a foundation's 990-PF returns incl. lot-level investment sales
    workbook built  : Financials tied across years; lot classification; excise tax; minimum distributable amount; payout and sale plan
    deliverable     : Payout and liquidity plan for the board
    checks vs source: Net-asset reconciliation and roll-forward; distributable amount recomputed
    judgment calls  : Lot gains that don't sum to the reported gain; which lots to sell; policy allocation
    closest videos  : 4CJHqj8bxAE, PDvt7L8U1Vg

* Chapter 11 cash monitoring  [#8 bankruptcy dockets; persona: Restructuring advisor]
    source documents: DIP budget, weekly variance reports, monthly operating reports (often scanned), claims register
    workbook built  : 13-week cash flow rolled forward, budget-to-actual variances, runway, claims by class, recovery waterfall
    deliverable     : Creditor-committee update with liquidity and recovery views
    checks vs source: Cash roll-forward ties to reported bank balances; cumulative variance vs covenant tolerance
    judgment calls  : Timing vs permanent variances; recovery assumptions by class
    closest videos  : ayNHfj3i9gI, hTAqXFQajRU, 8cigR2Ud6b8

* Pension accounting from actuarial reports  [#1 annual-report and actuarial PDFs; persona: Government accountant]
    source documents: Actuarial valuation and allocation schedules (copy-protected PDF), prior-year notes, contribution records
    workbook built  : Allocation factors keyed in, deferral schedules, pension liability roll-forward
    deliverable     : Year-end journal entries and note-disclosure tables
    checks vs source: Prior-year balances tie to prior notes; contributions reconcile to payment records
    judgment calls  : Measurement date vs fiscal year; plan-specific vs employer-specific deferrals
    closest videos  : lrorAMC4JJs (A), NU6EniOwjm0


----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Flagship long-horizon workflows (docs/03_source_catalog.md section 3)
----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
## 3. Flagship long-horizon workflows (from the user's seat)

Each is one *episode* a finance expert would recognise as "a big chunk of my job". Horizon
estimates are rough (documents × periods × concept steps) and meant to show scale, not to be
exact. Checkpoints marked [GT] have natural ground truth from the source.

### F1. Municipal credit review + annual surveillance (persona: muni credit analyst / rating analyst)
*Env:* one city: 5–10 ACFR PDFs, 1–2 official statements, latest actuarial valuation,
checkbook for the overlapping years; plus a 20-city peer set (CA SCO FTR or Census) for benchmarking.
1. Extract governmental-fund and government-wide statements for each year [GT: fund-balance
   roll-forward; gov-wide↔fund reconciliation schedule].
2. Normalise restatements, special items, fiscal-year changes (A4/A5).
3. Fund-balance and liquidity trend (H1); revenue concentration and intergovernmental reliance (H5).
4. Build the debt-service schedule from the OS; tie to the ACFR debt note [GT]; compute DSCR
   under the bond resolution's *net revenues* definition (not GAAP) (H3, C5).
5. Pension and OPEB: NPL roll-forward, ADC vs. actual contributions (H4) [GT: actuarial report vs. ACFR RSI].
6. Checkbook test: vendor totals by fund vs. ACFR expenditures; duplicate/split payments (J3) [GT: partial].
7. Peer percentile; memo with rating-factor scorecard (LLM-judged).
*Horizon:* ~10 long PDFs × 6–8 statements + 1–6M ledger rows → plausibly hundreds of actions.
*What separates models:* definitions that differ across documents (resolution vs. GAAP), restatements, and real reconciling items.

### F2. ABS surveillance desk, 24 months (persona: structured-credit surveillance analyst)
*Env:* one auto trust (or a shelf of 5): 24 monthly ABS-EE tapes (~10⁴–10⁵ loans each), 24 10-D
servicer reports (HTML), the 424B prospectus.
1. Each month: ingest tape, cut by FICO/LTV/term/state (F10) [GT: strata sum to pool].
2. Delinquency transition matrix and roll rates (F5); CPR/ABS speed, CDR, severity (F4).
3. Pool roll-forward and reconciliation to the 10-D [GT: pool balance, delinquencies, CNL].
4. Parse the prospectus waterfall and trigger definitions; replicate the month's distribution
   and test triggers (F6) [GT: 10-D distribution amounts].
5. Carry state: cumulative net loss curve vs. pricing assumption; projected trigger breach date.
6. Monthly note to the PM (LLM-judged); repeat for 24 periods with compounding state.
*Horizon:* 24 periods × 6 steps, and early mistakes (e.g., the recoveries double-count) poison later months.

### F3. Portfolio rebalancing (two variants)
F3a. US target-date fund due diligence + glide-path drift (persona: OCIO manager researcher).
N-PORT for a TDF family's vintages (8+ quarters) and their underlying index funds; prospectus
glide path; Fama-French factors. Look-through (E1) [GT: Σ = net assets] → return chaining vs.
reported (E2) [GT] → drift vs. glide path (E3) → attribution (E8) → fee drag (E11) → handle the
2022 Institutional→Investor merger break → recommendation.

F3b. India wealth desk quarterly review + rebalance (persona: mutual-fund distributor / RIA).
Client holds 8 schemes across 6 AMCs. Parse each AMC's raw workbook (different layouts)
[GT: Grand Total] → look-through by ISIN/sector/market-cap bucket → overlap matrix → SEBI
category-rule check → trailing return/vol from AMFI NAV → Regular→Direct switch via TER → tax-aware
trade list to a target allocation (E4, E5, K2) → client letter. Repeat monthly for 6 months (state:
lots, holding periods for LTCG).

### F4. Utility rate case (persona: regulatory analyst at a utility or a commission)
FERC Form 1 for one utility over 10 years + 15 peers. Rate-base roll-forward from plant in
service, accumulated depreciation, regulatory assets (B1, B6) [GT: 99.7% roll-forward; PUDL
rate-base table] → capital structure and cost of capital (C7) → revenue requirement → test-year
normalisation → O&M peer benchmarking → explain the cross-year breaks (restatements,
reclassifications) → testimony-style summary.

### F5. Chapter 11 creditor-committee monitoring (persona: restructuring financial advisor)
One case: first-day declaration, DIP budget, 30 weekly variance reports, 12 MORs (scanned),
claims register, disclosure statement. MOR OCR + cash roll-forward (I2) [GT: bank balances] →
cumulative DIP variance vs. covenant tolerance (I1) [GT] → liquidity runway (C4) → professional
fees vs. budget (I6) → claims reconciliation by class (I3) [GT: register totals] → recovery
waterfall vs. disclosure statement (I4) [GT] → committee update.

### F6. Private-foundation year-end close + payout plan (persona: foundation CFO / tax preparer)
990-PF for 5 years. Parse ~4k lot lines, classify ST/LT [GT: reported totals] → reconcile Σ lots to
net gain and explain gaps → excise tax → minimum distributable amount [GT] → join CUSIP holdings
to N-PORT/13F sectors → next-year sale plan via HIFO lots while rebalancing to policy.

---


----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Concept library: composition and worked compositions (docs/02_concept_library.md)
----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
## 3. Composition: how a "big" workflow emerges from concepts

Grammar. Workflow = Persona × Trigger × Env(sources, periods, entities) × DAG(concepts) × Checkpoints × Judgment points → Deliverable

The horizon of a workflow (roughly, number of agent actions) comes from five multipliers:

1. Acquisition burden: raw docs → usable tables (A-family) for every period and entity.
2. Concept depth: length of the DAG (e.g., rebalancing needs 7 to 9 concepts).
3. Temporal roll-forward: repeat the DAG for each period, carrying state (positions, balances, cumulative variances) forward. Errors compound.
4. Breadth: number of entities (a 40-fund universe, 120 municipalities).
5. Exceptions & judgment: definitions buried in legal text (credit agreement EBITDA, indenture triggers), restatements, missing months, corporate actions.

A source with high scores on (1), (3) *and* natural ground truth is the jackpot: long, hard, and still gradeable.

### Worked compositions (illustrations, sources detailed in the catalog)

W1. Quarterly portfolio rebalance (wealth/PM persona)
A1/A6 ingest holdings → E1 reconcile to NAV → (prices, corporate actions) → E3 drift vs. policy → E12 IPS constraints → E4 constrained trade list → E5 tax-lot selection → E6 cost estimate → E10 post-trade liquidity check → memo
Roll it over 8 quarters with real fund holdings (e.g., N-PORT / AMFI monthly portfolios), so state carries forward (lots, wash-sale windows).

W2. Municipal credit review (muni analyst persona)
A1 extract 5–10 years of ACFR statements (scanned/native PDF) → A4/A5 align & handle restatements → H1 fund balance trend → H2 gov-wide↔fund recon → H3 debt service schedule from Official Statement → H4 pension NPL/ADC → H5 revenue concentration → peer benchmarking → rating-style memo
Natural ground truth: ACFR's own reconciliation schedules and statistical section.

W3. Monthly ABS surveillance (structured-credit persona)
ingest loan-level tape (XML, ~10^4–10^5 loans) → F10 strats → F5 delinquency roll rates → F4 CPR/CDR/severity → B1 pool balance roll-forward → reconcile to servicer report (natural GT) → F6 waterfall & trigger tests vs. prospectus → forecast → surveillance note
Repeat for 12+ monthly periods; trigger breaches are real events.

W4. Chapter 11 case monitoring (restructuring advisor persona)
A1 OCR monthly operating reports → I2 cash roll-forward → I1 DIP budget variance (weekly) → C4 liquidity runway → I6 professional fees → I3 claims reconciliation → I4 recovery waterfall vs. disclosure statement → creditor-committee update

W5. Fiscal-year close & budget-to-actual from a government checkbook (controller/auditor persona)
A7 map transaction-level payments to fund/function → B3 cash→modified-accrual adjustments → H6 budget-to-actual → J3 duplicate/split payment tests → J1 sample for vouching → reconcile to ACFR fund statements (natural GT) → findings memo

W6. Bank peer surveillance (examiner/bank analyst persona)
ingest call reports (many quarters, many banks) → G1/G2 capital & liquidity → G3 allowance roll-forward → G4 NIM bridge → G6 CRE concentration → G5 peer percentiles → watchlist + memo

W7. Fund due diligence (manager-selection persona)
E1 holdings history → E2 reconstruct returns vs. reported → E8 attribution vs. benchmark → E9 style drift/overlap → E10 liquidity → E11 fee analysis from shareholder reports → IC memo

W8. Utility rate-case model (regulatory analyst persona)
extract regulatory accounts (FERC-style) → B1 rate-base roll-forward → C7 cost of capital → revenue requirement = rate base × ROR + O&M + depreciation + taxes → test-year adjustments → C1 variance vs. prior case → testimony-style summary

---


## 4. What a data source must offer for long-horizon scope (scoring lens)

| Criterion | Question to ask of a source | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Realness | Is it primary-source, unmodified real data? | goal requirement |
| Concept depth | How many families (A–K) can be exercised on the *same* records? | DAG length |
| Temporal depth | Many periods, same schema/entity? Refresh cadence? | roll-forward + contamination resistance |
| Breadth | Many comparable entities? | universe envs, screens, peers |
| Messiness | Scans, layout drift, units, restatements? | acquisition burden, realism |
| Natural ground truth | Does the source report totals that bottom-up work must hit? | verifiability of long trajectories |
| Join keys | CUSIP/ISIN/CIK/LEI/EIN/case no. to other sources | multi-source envs |
| Benchmark saturation | Already used by FinanceBench / SpreadsheetBench 2 / etc.? | differentiation |
| Scrapability | Bulk/API vs. brittle scraping; ToS; rate limits; mirrors | build cost |


====================================================================================================
12. CAVEATS
====================================================================================================

- Video coverage is uneven: funds (22) and public sector (23) searches completed; corporate (18) and credit (8)
  were stopped early (no bank or BDC videos written up).
- Video steps come from auto-generated captions read by research agents; numbers in captions can be garbled.
  Only the Otis reference was re-checked line by line against its transcript.
- Fit ratings (A/B/C) are judgment calls on whether a video shows the full chain.
- Three India videos have Hindi auto-captions only; one video (ZTgdYJF1rNY) has no captions; four videos have no
  length recorded (YouTube rate-limited the lookups).
- Data-source caveats: see section 4. Benchmark details marked "unverified" in the survey came from search
  snippets only (e.g. vals.ai, accounting.penrose.com, spreadsheetbench.github.io were blocked).
- The 'same-level' workflows in section 11 are proposals assembled from partial videos and our PoCs; none exists
  as a single end-to-end video.
